<header>The First Epistle.</header>

[...that no one can] inherit or possess the Kingdom of God within it. Nor is God manifest in any such desire, but only his anger and the nature of the dark and earthly world. We live there only for the vanity of this world and stand with it only upon the abyss of the dark world and the anger of God—that is, of Hell. Hell continually gapes and hungers for these qualities, counting them its own fruit and children, which it shall reap and take into its barn; for they belong to it by the right of nature. These desires all originate from it and have their root in the ground of Hell and destruction, and indeed nowhere else.

67. Therefore Christ said, *Unless a man be born anew, he shall not see the Kingdom of God.* All these false wills and desires are predestined to damnation. If anyone wishes to see God, he must be converted and become like a child, being born again in the Holy Ghost through the water of eternal life—that is, through the heavenly <term>Ens</term> <note>original: Latin for "essence" or "being"; here referring to the spiritual substance of Christ</note> which God has revealed in Christ. This is so that the first true man, who died in Adam (originating from the essence of the heavenly worlds), might spring forth again in Christ and become living.

68. All these evil beasts <note>Referring to the internal vices and selfish desires discussed on the previous page.</note> are condemned and must die in us. Although their desire clings and sticks to us in the flesh, they must all be mortified <note>put to death or subdued</note> during this <gloss>[ life ]</gloss> time in the soul—namely, in the inward ground. The inward ground of the soul must be <term>Tinctured</term> <note>A term from alchemy used here to mean "transformed" or "permeated" with a divine quality.</note> by the true <term>Tincture</term> in the blood of Christ, so that the properties of the internal ground may again live in harmony and concord; otherwise, they cannot reach the Deity within themselves.

<margin>☞</margin>
69. Now when a person knows this, they can find no better way to be rid of their evil, beast-like desires than to immediately, at that very instant, commit themselves with all their strength to a strong will and earnest purpose. They must decide that from now on they will hate and abandon these evil beasts of the Devil, seeing as they are only the Devil's servants. They must return to their lost country, to the adoption <note>the state of being a child of God</note> and atonement, and view themselves as nothing other than the lost swineherd <gloss>[ or the prodigal son ]</gloss>. For the person is himself the lost swineherd and no better at all. He should immediately approach the Father with the conversion of his soul in the deepest humility, acknowledging his unworthiness—that he has so shamefully and unworthily misspent and misused the free inheritance of Christ’s merits—and so enter into repentance.

<margin>☞</margin>
70. Let him give up his earnest will to this with all his strength: that from this very time forward, he will repent and amend, and no longer love these evil beasts. But this must be in earnest, in real sincerity, without delays, and not thinking of a day, a week...

<summary>Boehme argues that human vices are "beastly" qualities belonging to the abyss of Hell and must be purged through a spiritual rebirth in Christ. He calls for an immediate, sincere repentance, comparing the sinner to the Prodigal Son returning to the Father.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Boehme’s discussion of the soul's internal struggle, specifically how the mind must reject the "beastly" vices mentioned previously and turn toward God’s mercy.</meta>

...or year; but his mind must utterly and continually condemn them to the damnation of death, and not will to love them any more, but count them enemies, and so resolve to turn himself to the mercy and grace of God.

71. When this is done (I speak as I have known it deeply in the precious light of grace), then he may turn himself to earnest praying in humility and beseech God for grace. And though his heart says utterly, "no," and the **Devil** says, "stay yet a while; it is not now good or convenient; you will have better opportunity tomorrow," and when tomorrow comes, he says again, "tomorrow," and suggests to the flesh, saying, "You must first do this or that; first gather a treasure for yourself so that you do not need the world, and then enter into such a life"—yet I say, the mind must remain firm and steadfast in its purpose. It must think to itself, "These suggestions and thoughts are my evil, hungry beasts; these I will kill and drown in the blood of **Christ's** love. None of them shall live any longer in me; I will have no more to do with them. I am now on the way to my ancient Father, who has sent his Son to me in my misery and distress, saying: *Come to me, all you that are weary [grieved] or heavy laden with sins, and I will refresh you.* <note>A reference to Matthew 11:28.</note> My Father will give the Holy Spirit to those who ask him for it."

<margin>He must imprint and settle this in his resolution.</margin>
72. Let him imagine and *impress this into his very heart, and come with the lost Son <note>A reference to the Parable of the Prodigal Son in Luke 15.</note> to the Father.* And when the Father sees that the mind of the soul is directed toward him, and would willingly and sincerely turn and yet cannot, then He will immediately meet him and clasp the soul into the arms of his drawing <note>God's "drawing" refers to the magnetic pull of divine grace toward the Creator.</note> and bring her into the passion and death of **Christ**. There, through earnest repentance and sorrow, she will die from (or <term>mortify</term> <note>original: "mortifie"; to put to death or subdue the physical or sinful desires</note>) those abominable beasts and arise out of the death of **Christ** with a new will and a true divine desire. Then he will begin to be another man indeed, and will not at all value that which he before loved and held as his treasure. It will be to him as if he had it, and also as if he had it not; and afterward, he will account himself in all his power, ability, and possessions but as a servant and steward of God.

73. For as soon as he is able to master and overcome the proud **Lucifer** of pride, all the other evil beasts will grow weak and faint and lose their ruling power and dominion. Although they still live during this <gloss>[ life ]</gloss> time in the earthly flesh, they are only as an ass which must carry the sack, or as a mad dog on a chain; their strength is broken.

74. For when **Christ** rises, **Lucifer** must lie captive; and if there is an earnest, full perseverance, such a precious jewel would follow...

<summary>Boehme describes the difficult process of spiritual conversion, emphasizing the need for the soul to reject worldly distractions and "inner beasts" through prayer and Christ's grace. He explains that by subduing pride, the believer becomes a steward of God, holding earthly possessions lightly while the "beastly" nature is rendered powerless.</summary>

...as this pen cannot describe here; and those alone know it who have been at the heavenly marriage, where the noble <term>Sophia</term> <note>original: "Sophia"; the Greek word for Wisdom, representing the divine feminine principle or the Wisdom of God in Boehme’s theology.</note> is espoused to the soul. Concerning this, Christ said that there is greater joy in heaven over one sinner who repents than over ninety-nine righteous people. This joy is likewise in the "heaven of man" during this espousal or marriage; this is understood by our <term>schoolfellows</term> <note>original: "Schoole-fellowes"; Boehme's term for fellow spiritual seekers or those initiated into these divine mysteries.</note>.

75. Dear Sir and Christian fellow-brother, I thought it good (meaning well toward you in a Christian and sincere manner) to remind you of this and to set it forth from my <term>little treasury</term> <note>original: "little Treasury"; referring to Boehme's internal store of spiritual insights and revelations.</note> in a childlike simplicity. I do not intend by this to show off or promote myself, but I act out of a true and hearty desire, wishing that this might be felt in your heart as well. I also wish that I might rejuvenate and refresh myself a little with you as a fellow member—though absent in body, yet present in desire and cooperating in the divine gifts—and I do this at your request, as was said before.

76. And if my goodwill should find a place, and God would open the door of his mystery, then I might perhaps have some other more precious jewels in my little treasury in which Time and Eternity may be known. I am ready and willing to offer you my service in this regard. And so I commend you and yours to the sweet and pleasant love, grace, and will of *Jesus Christ*.

<summary>Boehme describes the ecstatic "heavenly marriage" between the soul and Divine Wisdom (Sophia) and concludes his letter by offering further spiritual guidance from his personal insights.</summary>

<page-num>18</page-num>
<header>The Second Epistle.</header>

# A Letter to
## CASPAR LINDERN,
### <term>Customer</term> <note>original: "Customer"; a customs official or tax collector.</note> at *BEUTEN* <note>Modern Bytom, Poland.</note>.

---
->WHEREIN<-
->The plain and simple way is described,<-
->which the Author took for the achievement<-
->of his high Knowledge:<-
---
->**A L S O,**<-
---
->His assessment, judgment, and answer<-
->concerning various authors of different<-
->opinions, tending to lead Christians<-
->into the excellent and desired way<-
->of Love and Union.<-
---

<margin>1.</margin>
> **T**HE open fountain in the heart of Jesus Christ refresh us and lead us to himself, so that we may live in his power and rejoice in him; so that we may love and understand one another and enter into one single **Will.**

2. Much respected and discreet Sir, my most worthy friend in the love and humanity of *Jesus Christ*: my hearty desires from God in our *<term>Immanuel</term>* <note>original: "Immanuel"; Hebrew for "God with us."</note> for prosperity upon soul and body are put forward first. I give you, Sir, to understand that I have received your letter, and therein perceive that you are a seeker and great lover of the Mystery—that is, of the knowledge of God—and that you diligently take care to pick up some divine crumbs everywhere, bearing likewise a great desire and hunger after them.

3. Which, for my part, does highly rejoice me, that God does

<meta>This page continues the Second Epistle of Jacob Boehme to Caspar Lindern. Boehme moves from describing the communal spiritual bond between believers to his personal testimony of how he, an uneducated man, received his profound divine insights.</meta>

<page-num>19</page-num>
<header>The Second Epistle.</header>
<sig>D 2</sig>

thus draw and lead his children; as it is written: *Those who are led by the Spirit of God are the Children of God.* And as one branch on a tree rejoices in the other, and they mutually provide sap and assistance to one another, so likewise do the children of God in their Tree, *Jesus Christ*. And at this, my humble self <note>original: "my simple person"</note> does exceedingly rejoice: that God, from the fountain of his heart, draws us (as the simple children of our Mother) to himself—even to the right breast and bosom of our Mother—so that we should long after him, as children long for their mother.

4. And whereas (my beloved Sir and brother in the love of Christ) I see and perceive that you thirst after the open wellspring of Christ and likewise enjoy the same according to the will of God, yet you still inquire after the enjoyment of your brethren. You desire (as a branch on the Tree) to mutually recreate, refresh, and satisfy yourself in them. It is also acceptable to me to impart my sap and my spirit (in the knowledge which *God has given me*) unto my brethren and members—who are my fellow-branches in the Tree, *Jesus Christ*—and so to rejoice in them, namely, in their sap, power, and spirit. For it is the pleasant food of my soul to perceive that my fellow-branches and members flourish in the Paradise of God.

5. But I will not conceal from you the simple, childlike way which I walk in *Christ Jesus*. For I can write nothing of myself, but only as a child who neither knows nor understands anything, and has never been taught, except for that which the Lord graciously permits me to know <note>original: "vouchsafeth to know in me"</note>, according to the measure in which he manifests himself in me.

<margin>☞</margin>
6. For I never desired to know anything of the Divine Mystery, much less did I understand the way to seek or find it. I knew nothing of it, as is the condition of poor <term>Laymen</term> <note>original: "Lay-men"; common people who are not members of the clergy or formally trained in theology.</note> in their simplicity. I sought only after the heart of *Jesus Christ*, so that I might hide myself there from the wrathful anger of God and the violent assaults of the Devil. I besought the Lord earnestly for his Holy Spirit and his grace, that he would be pleased to bless and guide me in him, and take away from me that which turned me away from him. I resigned myself wholly to him, so that I might not live for my own will but for his, and that he alone might lead and direct me, to the end that I might be his child in his Son *Jesus Christ*.

7. In this, my earnest Christian seeking and desire (during which I suffered many a <note>original: "shrewd repulse"; a harsh or severe setback.</note> harsh setback, but at last was resolved to put my life to the utmost hazard rather than give over and leave off), the Gate was opened to me. In fifteen minutes <note>original: "one quarter of an hour"</note>, I saw and knew more than if I had been for many years together at a University.

<summary>Jacob Boehme explains that his spiritual knowledge is a gift from God, granted not through academic study but through a total surrender of his will and an earnest seeking of Christ's heart.</summary>

I knew and saw in myself all three worlds: namely, the divine, angelic, and paradisiacal <gloss>world</gloss>; then the dark world, being the origin of nature in the fire; and then thirdly, the eternal and visible world, being a procreation or external birth, or as a substance expressed or spoken aloud from both the internal and spiritual worlds. I saw and knew the whole Being <gloss>or working essence</gloss> in both evil and good, and the mutual origin and existence of each of them; and likewise how the * Pregnant Mother <note>the creative power of eternity</note> brought them forth, so that I did not only wonder at it greatly, but also rejoiced exceedingly.

9. And immediately it came powerfully into my mind to set this down in writing as a memorial to myself. Although I could only with great difficulty grasp it in my external man <note>the physical, rational self</note> and express it with the pen, I nevertheless had to begin laboring in these great mysteries like a child who goes to school. I saw it <gloss>as in a great deep</gloss> within, for I had a thorough view of the universe as if in a CHAOS <note>the primordial state of existence where all possibilities are present but unformed</note> in which all things are contained and wrapped up, but it was impossible for me to explain and unfold it.

<margin>
† Or, the breeding of it.
☞
</margin>
10. Yet it opened itself in me from time to time, like a young plant. Although it stayed with me for twelve years and I was, as it were, † pregnant with it and found a powerful drive and urge within me before I could bring it forth into an external form of writing, it afterward fell upon me like a sudden shower which hits whatever it lands on. Just so it happened to me: whatever I could grasp and bring into the external <gloss>principle of my mind</gloss>, I wrote down.

11. However, afterward the Sun did shine on me for a good while, but not in a continually constant manner. For when it hid itself, I scarcely knew or well understood my own labor <gloss>or writings</gloss>. Thus, a person must acknowledge that his knowledge is not his own or from himself, but God’s and from God; and that God knows <gloss>or manifests the ideas of His wisdom</gloss> in the soul of man in whatever manner and measure He pleases.

12. I intended to keep this writing of mine with me all the days of my life and not to deliver it into anyone's hands; but it happened according...

<meta>catchword: according</meta>

<summary>Boehme describes a profound mystical vision where he perceived the "Bysse" and "Abyss," the origins of the three worlds, and the nature of good and evil. He explains that his writings were not products of his own intellect but were "sudden showers" of divine inspiration revealed to him over twelve years.</summary>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues Jacob Boehme’s account of his spiritual journey and the aftermath of his first written work, *Aurora*. He describes a period of silence and persecution before a second wave of divine inspiration led him to write more deeply about the nature of existence.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>The Second Epistle.</header>

...according to the providence of the Most High, I entrusted a certain person with some of it. By means of this, it was published and made known without my knowledge or consent. As a result, the first book (called *The Dawn* <note>original: "Aurora"</note>) was * taken from me <margin>* By Gregory Richter, Lord Primate of Görlitz.</margin>; and because many wonderful things were revealed in it (which the human mind was not immediately able to comprehend), I was forced <note>original: "faint," here meaning obliged or compelled</note> to suffer much from Reason <note>Boehme often personifies "Reason" as the limited human intellect that opposes spiritual revelation</note>.

13. I did not see this first book again for three years; I supposed that it was dead and gone, until certain learned men sent me some copies of it. They urged me to proceed and manifest my talent. To this, my outward reason would by no means agree, because it had suffered so much already for it. Moreover, the spirit of reason was very weak and fearful, for my "High Light" <note>Boehme's term for his state of divine illumination</note> was also withdrawn from me for a good while. It glowed in me like a hidden fire, so that I felt nothing but anguish and perplexity within me. Outwardly I found contempt, and inwardly a fiery prompting; yet I was not able to comprehend [that light] until the breath [or inspiration] of the Most High helped me to it again and awakened new life in me. Then I obtained a better style in writing, as well as deeper and more grounded knowledge. I could express everything better in outward words, which the book treating of the † *Threefold Life* through the Three Principles <term>Boehme’s cosmological system consisting of the dark-fire world, the light world, and the visible world</term> <margin>† Or, According to.</margin> demonstrates; and the godly reader, whose heart is opened, shall see [that it is so].

14. Thus I have now written, not from the instruction or knowledge received from men, nor from the learning or reading of books; but I have written out of my own book which was opened within me. This book is the noble similitude <term>likeness or image</term> of God. The book of the noble and precious Image (meaning God’s own likeness) was bestowed upon me to read; and I have studied it as a child in its mother's house, who watches what the father does and, in his childlike play, imitates his father. I have no need of any other book.

<margin>Note what a person can find in themselves. ☞</margin>
15. My book has only three leaves <note>original: "leaves," referring to the pages or sections of a book</note>; these are the three Principles of Eternity. In them, I can find everything that *Moses* and the prophets, *Christ* and his *Apostles* have taught and spoken. I can find there the foundation of the world and all mysteries—yet it is not I who does this, but the Spirit of God, according to the measure that pleases him.

16. For I have besought and begged of him many hundreds of times that if my knowledge did not serve his glory and contribute to the * improvement <margin>* Bettering or benefit.</margin> and instruction of my brothers, he would be pleased to take it from me and preserve me only in his love. Yet I found that by my praying or earnest desiring, I only ignited...

<summary>Boehme recounts the confiscation of his first book, Aurora, by church authorities and his subsequent period of spiritual withdrawal. He eventually returned to writing, claiming his insights come not from academic study but from a divine "internal book" representing the image of God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jacob Boehme’s explanation of his writing process. He describes his work as a product of divine inspiration rather than human intellect, positioning himself as a simple instrument of God’s will.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>The Second Epistle.</header>

...the fire more strongly in me; and in such inflammation, knowledge, and manifestation, I produced my writings.

17. Yet I did not intend to make myself known through them among such people as I now see has happened; I still thought I wrote for myself alone. Although the spirit of God—within the Mystery of God in my own spirit—clearly showed me for what purpose it was intended, outward Reason <term>the limited human intellect</term> was always opposed, except occasionally when the Morning Star <note>A symbol of divine enlightenment or the dawning of Christ's presence in the soul</note> arose. Even then, Reason was also ignited by it and danced along as if it had comprehended [the Pearl], yet it was far from doing so.

<margin>☞</margin> 18. God dwells in the Noble Image <term>the divine spark or the original purity of the soul</term>, but not in the spirit of the stars and elements. He possesses nothing but himself alone, in his own likeness; and although he does possess all things (as indeed he possesses everything), nothing comprehends him except what originally arises and springs from him—namely, the soul in the likeness of God.

19. Furthermore, all my writings are like those of a young scholar who is going to school. God has, according to his will, brought my soul into a wonderful school; and in truth, I cannot credit or claim <note>original: "arrogate"</note> anything for myself, as if my own self-hood <note>original: "selfehood," meaning his individual ego or independent identity</note> existed or understood anything on its own.

<margin>☞</margin> 20. No man must think more highly of me than what he sees; for the work in my studying or writing is not my own. I have it only according to the measure that the Lord is pleased to give it to me. I am nothing but his instrument, by which he accomplishes what he wills. I tell you this, my beloved friends, for your instruction and information, lest anyone should esteem me as something other than what I am—namely, as if I were a man of great learning <note>original: "high art"</note>, deep understanding, and reason. For I live in weakness and infirmity, in the innocence <note>original: "childhood"</note> and simplicity of Christ. My joy and activity <note>original: "sport and pastime"</note> is in that child-like work which he has assigned to me. Indeed, I take delight in it as if in a pleasure garden where many noble flowers grow; and in the meantime, I will rejoice and refresh myself with it until I once again obtain the Flower of Paradise <term>a state of spiritual perfection or divine grace</term> in the "New Man."

21. But because, dear Sir and beloved friend, I see and perceive that you are seeking this path, I write to you with diligence regarding my child-like course. I understand that you make use of various authors and writings concerning which you desire my judgment. I shall share this with you as my fellow-member <note>A fellow member of the spiritual body of Christ</note>, as far as God has given me to know, and only in a brief and short summary. In my book *The Threefold Life*, you shall find it explained at length, according to all circumstances.

22. And this is the answer <note>original: "Answer"</note> I give to you: namely <note>original: "Viz."</note>, that self-reason (which, being void of God’s spirit, is only taught and instructed...

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>The Second Epistle.</header>

(judging from the literal word alone) <note>original: "from the bare Letter"</note> cavils, taunts, derides, and despises whatever does not punctually agree and conform to the canons and institutions of the universities and high schools. I do not wonder at this, for such judgment comes from the outside, while God’s spirit is from within. This worldly reason contains both good and evil; it is like the wind, which is moved and driven to and fro. It <margin>Highly prizes.</margin> *highly prizes* man’s judgment; and according to how the high and great ones—who have the respect and authority of the world—judge and censure, it gives its credit and verdict accordingly. It does not know the mind of the Lord, because that mind is not in it. Its understanding is from the stars <note>In Boehme’s philosophy, "starry" or "astral" knowledge refers to the natural human intellect, which is inferior to divine revelation</note>, and it is nothing else but a counterfeit shadow of fancy in comparison to divine wisdom.

<margin>☛</margin> 23. How can he judge of divine matters in whom the spirit of the Lord is not? The spirit of the Lord alone tries, proves, and judges all things, for to him only all things are known and manifest. But *Reason* judges outwardly, and one *Reason* always squares its judgment and opinion according to another. The inferior person judges and censures just as his great superior does—the layman as the *Doctor* <note>original: "Doctor," referring to a highly educated theologian or scholar</note>—and yet neither of them apprehends the sense, mind, and truth of the Lord without the spirit of God, which judges in man and respects no man’s person. To the spirit, the layman and the *Doctor* are both one and the same.

<margin>☛</margin> 24. Now, whereas the children of God have diverse and manifold gifts in writing, speaking, and judging, they do not all have the same manner of expression, phrase, and style. Because of this, "self-reason" <note>intellect motivated by the ego rather than the spirit</note> afterward—through artificial conclusions—draws out of these writings whatever serves its own purpose and builds a *Babel* <note>a reference to the Tower of Babel, symbolizing spiritual confusion and the "clatter" of conflicting religious dogmas</note> for itself. From this, such a multitude and wearisome heap of opinions have arisen, as men have forged and invented diverse conjectures and ways to God out of these writings. Men are then forced to walk in those ways, which is how such controversies and un-Christian contentions have arisen. At present, men look only at the strife of words and disputes about the literal text. Those who—according to their reason and principles—overcome others by verbal quarreling and by exchanging one scripture for another are applauded. But this is nothing but *Babel*, a mother of spiritual whoredom, where *Reason* does not enter in at the door of *Christ* through *Christ’s* spirit, but presses in of itself. It climbs up by its own might, strength, and pride, while still remaining a stranger or unregenerated; it would always gladly be the fairest child in the house, demanding that men honor and adore it.

<margin>☛</margin> 25. The children of God have a diversity of gifts, according to the rule of the Apostle; God gives an expression to everyone as he pleases. The gifts and endowments of men fall out according to the...

<summary>Boehme argues that true spiritual understanding comes from the indwelling Spirit of God rather than academic training or the literal interpretation of scripture. He warns that human "Reason" creates "Babel"—a confusion of conflicting opinions and religious strife—by trying to master divine truths through intellectual pride.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Boehme’s explanation of how spiritual knowledge is revealed. He argues that the soul’s understanding depends on its internal spiritual alignment—its "constellation"—within the divine structure.</meta>

<page-num>24</page-num>
<header>The Second Epistle.</header>

...the unsearchable will of God, and spring altogether out of one Root, which is the Mother of the <term>Three Principles</term> <note>Boehme’s concept of the three "worlds" or "natures": the dark fire-world (the Father), the light world (the Son), and the outer physical world (the Spirit/Nature).</note>. Just as the spirit of every soul is <term>constellated</term> <note>original: "Constellated"; here meaning arranged or influenced by a specific spiritual map or internal pattern.</note> in the eternal Mother, so too is its revelation, understanding, and knowledge.

<margin>☞</margin> 26. For God does not bring a new or strange Spirit into us; rather, he opens our own spirit with his Spirit—namely, the Mystery of God's Wisdom, which lies in every man according to the measure, manner, and condition of his internal, hidden constellation. For Christ said, "*My Father works, and I also work*" <note>John 5:17</note>. Now the Father works in the essence of the soul’s unique property, and the Son works in the essence of God’s own image—that is, in the divine likeness or harmony.

27. The property of the soul belongs to the Father; for Christ said, "*Father, the men were yours, and you have given them to me, and I give to them eternal life*" <note>John 17:6</note>. Since the property of the soul is from eternity, of and from the Father, he has therefore worked in it from eternity, and still works in that same image toward eternity—in both light and darkness—to whichever the *will* of the soul's nature inclines and surrenders itself.

28. Seeing then that the Father's property or wisdom is immeasurable and infinite, and that he, being the wisdom itself, works—and yet through his wisdom all things arise—consequently, the souls of men are diversely "constellated" <note>influenced by different spiritual characteristics or "stars"</note>. Indeed, they arise and originally proceed out of only one essence, yet the operation is diverse and manifold, all according to God's wisdom. Now the spirit of Christ opens the property of every soul, so that each speaks from its own property about the wonders in the wisdom of God.

<margin>☞</margin> 29. For the spirit of God makes no new thing in man, nor does it infuse a strange spirit into him; rather, he speaks of the wonders in the wisdom of God through man. This happens not from the eternal constellation alone, but likewise from the external constellation; that is, through the spirit of the external world, he opens the internal constellation of the soul in man, so that he must prophesy and foretell what the external heaven works and produces. Also, he is driven to speak through the <term>Great Disturbance</term> <note>original: "Turba Magna"; Boehme’s term for the divine wrath or the chaotic force of judgment that breaks things apart.</note>, just as the Prophets have many times spoken and denounced to the people their punishment which, by God's permission, should come upon them through the <term>Great Disturbance</term> for their violence and sinfulness. [And their bitter curses, wicked contentions, and wrathful indignation in their envious will against one another awaken the <term>sword of anger</term> in the Great Disturbance.]

<margin>☞</margin> 30. Now the spirit of God speaks in his children in various ways; sometimes in one person it speaks by the internal and eternal—

<summary>Jacob Boehme explains that divine revelation is the awakening of a person's innate spiritual "constellation" by God's Spirit, rather than an external infusion. He describes how the soul's nature relates to the Father and Son, and how prophets are moved to speak warnings through the "Great Disturbance" (Turba Magna).</summary>

<meta>This page continues Boehme’s explanation of how the "internal constellation" of the soul determines its spiritual reception. He distinguishes between genuine divine revelation and the deceptive influences of the physical universe (the astral spirit) and human reason.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>
<header>The Second Epistle.</header>

<margin>☞</margin>...eternal <term>constellation</term> <note>original: "Constellation"; here meaning the internal spiritual arrangement or map of the soul</note> of the soul, regarding eternal punishment or reward, and God's curse or blessing. In another sense, it speaks through the external constellation about the fortune or misfortune, the prosperity or adversity of this world. It also speaks of the rise and advancement of powers and authorities, and then likewise of the ruin and destruction of countries and cities, as well as strange and wonderful changes in the world.

31. And though it happens often that the spirit of the outward world plays with its own images of imagination within man, and by its own power and strong influence creeps into the spirit of man to show various strange and marvelous figures—which only find a place among those who follow their own human reason in proud self-will, which is how <margin>☜</margin> false prophets arise—yet I say that everyone speaks from their own constellation. One speaks through the manifestation of God’s Spirit, truly and sincerely; the other speaks through the manifestation of the external *astral* <note>original: "Astral"; referring to the spirit of the physical stars and the natural world, which Boehme believes can influence the human mind</note> spirit, unclearly and by guesswork. Both speak from the same constellation. However, he who speaks from the mouth of another and likewise judges the *mystery* without personal knowledge is in <term>Babel</term> <note>original: "Babel"; a reference to the Tower of Babel, representing a state of spiritual confusion, empty intellectualism, and sectarianism</note>. He is entangled in opinion, willfully occupying himself with things that his heart has not experienced as true; [ but he simply *pins his faith upon the sayings of other men.* ]

32. And I say further that all those precious men who have been enlightened by God (some of whose writings you may have at hand) have spoken from their own manifestation and revelation. Each spoke according to his own understanding or the measure of his capacity. Yet the center is the soul, and the light is God. The revelation is accomplished and brought to pass by the opening or manifestation of God’s Spirit through the constellation of the soul.

<margin>☞</margin> 33. All the prophets from the beginning of the world have prophesied of Christ in different forms—one this way, another that way. They have not all agreed in one style, phrase, and form; instead, each spoke according to how the Spirit of God revealed it to him in the eternal constellation of the soul. Yet they all spoke from one center and foundation. And so it is nowadays: the children of God all speak from the revelation of Christ’s Spirit, which is God’s; and everyone speaks according to their capacity, or that idea of wisdom which is formed in their mind. Therefore, I remind you as a friend and exhort you not to listen to the empty babbling and chatter of reason. Do not be moved by its proud criticism and judgment so as to condemn or despise any man's gifts; for he who does so insults the Spirit of God.

<page-num>26</page-num>
# The Second Epistle.

<margin>☛</margin>
34. Regarding these authors whom you mention, and others besides (about whom you desire my judgment, though I have not read all of them, but only in part), I do not desire to judge or despise them—God forbid! Let that be far from me, even though they have not all written in the same style and form of expression. For knowledge is diverse and manifold. Yet it is necessary for me to test their hearts and wills according to my own gifts. Since I find that their hearts and spirits flow and spring from one and the same center—namely, from the spirit of Christ—I am therefore content to focus on that center. I commend their expression to the <term>Highest Tongue</term> <note>original: "Highest Tongue"; referring to the Spirit of God's wisdom</note>, that is, to the spirit of God's wisdom. Through that wisdom, God opens and reveals Himself to everyone according to the measure and manner that pleases Him.

35. I judge no one, and to condemn anyone is a false and idle arrogance and empty <term>prating</term> <note>original: "prating"; foolish or irrelevant talk</note>. The spirit of God Himself judges all things. If that spirit is in us, why should we care for idle talk? I much rather rejoice at the gifts of my brothers. If they have had a different manner of gifts to present than I have, should I therefore judge them?

36. Does any herb, flower, or tree say to another, "You are sour and dark; I will not stand by you"? Do they not all have one Mother from whom they grow? Even so, all souls proceed from One, and all men from one. Why then do we boast and glory in being the children of God, when we are more unwise than the flowers and herbs of the field? Is it not the same with us? Does not God impart and reveal His wisdom to us in diverse ways? Just as He brings forth and manifests the <term>Tincture</term> <note>original: "Tincture"; in Boehme’s thought, a spiritual essence or life-force that gives quality and color to physical things</note> of the <term>Mystery</term> <note>original: "Mystery"; the *Mysterium Magnum*, or the hidden divine source of all creation</note> in the earth through the soil with beautiful plants, He does the same in us humans. We should instead congratulate and heartily love one another because God reveals His wisdom so variously within us. But he who judges, condemns, and despises in a wicked way only runs on in pride to show himself off and be seen. He is the **Oppressor in Babel** <note>original: "Oppressour in Babel"; a person who contributes to spiritual confusion and the "tower" of human intellectual pride</note>, a perverse <term>stickler</term> <note>original: "stickler"; here meaning a contentious person who stirs up trouble over small details</note> who stirs up contention and strife.

<margin>☛</margin>
37. The true <term>trial</term> <note>original: "Tryall"; the test or proof of genuine faith</note> of God's children is this, which we may securely and safely follow: namely, an humble heart that neither seeks nor honors itself, but continually seeks the good of its brother in love. It does not seek after its own profit, pleasure, and applause, but after righteousness and the fear of God. The plain and simple way to come to God is this (so far as it has been made known to me): that a person departs from their sinful courses and makes an earnest, constant purpose never to continue any longer in those sins they have committed; and in their forsaking and turning away from them, not to despair or doubt God's grace.

<summary>Boehme argues against judging other spiritual writers, asserting that divine wisdom is expressed diversely through different people just as nature produces various plants. He emphasizes that the true test of a child of God is humility, love for others, and a sincere turning away from sin.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jakob Boehme's guidance on spiritual enlightenment. He moves from discussing the diversity of spiritual gifts to the necessity of total surrender ("resignation") to God, comparing the believer's relationship with Divine Wisdom to that of a child with its mother.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>
# The Second Epistle.

<margin>☛</margin>
38. **And although reason suggests doubts (by which a sinner is terrified, and stands amazed and astonished at the anger of God)**, yet let the will only, in all simplicity and unfeigned sincerity, directly throw itself into the mercy of God. Let it lie down completely and shelter itself in the suffering and death of Christ, surrendering itself to God through Christ—just as a child betakes itself to the lap of the mother, wanting to do only that which is the mother's will. It only cries and calls to the mother, it always hopes to receive its refreshment from the mother, and it only longs after the mother's breasts. Even so must our desire be wholly and only turned and directed to our first Mother <note>Boehme frequently uses the "Mother" as a metaphor for Divine Wisdom or the "Eternal Word," the original source of the soul before the Fall.</note>, from whom we departed in *Adam* when we entered into *Self-Will*.

39. Therefore **Christ** says, *Unless you are converted and become as children, you cannot see the Kingdom of God*. Also, you must be born again—that is, we must wholly renounce and depart from our own reason, and come again into resignation <term>resignation</term> <note>original: "resignation [and selfe-denyall]"; in Boehme's theology, this refers to "Gelassenheit," the total yielding of the human will to God.</note> into the bosom of our Mother, and give up all disputing. We must, as <margin>☛</margin> it were, silence or deaden our reason, so that the spirit of the Mother (namely, of the *Eternal Word of God*) may take shape in us and blow up, or enkindle, the Divine life in us. In this way, we may find ourselves in the spirit of the Mother, as if in a cradle, if we desire to be taught and led by God.

40. And if we will be taught and led by God, then we must arise again from the cradle and wholly submit and give ourselves up to him, so that God's spirit may be in us *wholly, both the will and the deed*. Thus, we may acknowledge the knowledge to be His, and not ours; that He only may be our *Knowing*.

<margin>☛</margin>
41. **We must take no thought, or anxious care, about what we are to know and how we will know it. Instead, we must simply enter into the Incarnation and birth of Jesus Christ, and into his suffering and death, and continually with all willingness tread in his footsteps. We must follow him and remember that we are here only upon a pilgrim’s path, where we must walk through a dangerous way and enter again through Christ on the narrow way into our native country, from which Adam has led us astray. In this way alone lies the *Pearl* of the *Great Mystery* <note>original: "Mysterium magnum"</note>. All studying, book-reading, seeking, searching, and grounding ourselves on traditional principles or orthodox understandings—if done besides and without this way—are but dead means. They do not obtain the *Virgin’s Crown* <note>original: "Virgins Crowne [or the Pearle of Sophia]"; Sophia is the Greek word for Wisdom, personified in mystical tradition as the bride of the soul.</note> but instead gather together heaps of thistles and thorns, which sting and gall the children of God.**

42. Therefore, dear sir, seeing you have desired my knowledge and judgment, I have no better counsel and advice to impart to you than to show you the way which I myself walk in; and

<summary>Boehme advises the reader to abandon intellectual disputation and human reason in favor of a childlike, total surrender to God's will. He emphasizes that true spiritual knowledge (the "Pearl of the Great Mystery") comes only through following Christ's path of suffering and resignation, rather than through academic study or dogma.</summary>

# The Second Epistle.

...In this way the Gate was opened to me, so that I have become learned without prior schooling; for all *arts* and *sciences* come from God. He discovers all things [in, and for humanity.]

<margin>* Note this carefully.</margin>
<margin>☛</margin>
43. I have no controversy with the children of God because of the variety and diversity of their gifts; *I can reconcile them all within myself [I can interpret and understand them clearly for myself].* I only bring them back to the center; and there I find the proof and touchstone for all things. Now then, if you will imitate and follow me, you shall find this to be true through experience; and afterward, perhaps you will better understand what I have written.

<margin>☛</margin>
44. A real, true Christian has no controversy or contention with anyone, for through resignation <term>resignation</term> <note>original: "resignation in Christ"; the total yielding of the human will to the divine, a central concept in Boehme's "Gelassenheit."</note> in Christ, he dies to all controversy and strife. He no longer asks about the way to God, but wholly surrenders himself to the Mother—namely, to the Spirit of Christ. Whatever the Spirit does with him is all the same to him; whether it be prosperity or adversity in this world, life or death, it is all alike to him. No adversity or calamity reaches the *new man*, but only the old man of this world. The world may do what it pleases with that old man, for he belongs to the world; but the *new man* belongs to God.

<margin>☛</margin>
45. This is my way, my dear friend, in which I walk, and in which I must know things without prior study. I do not plan, premeditate, or muse beforehand on what I am to write or speak; instead, I submit and resign myself to the knowledge of God, so that He may know through me whatever He pleases. In such a way as this, I have obtained a pearl <note>original: "Pearle"; a reference to the "pearl of great price" from Matthew 13:45-46, representing the kingdom of heaven or ultimate spiritual truth.</note> which I value more than the entire external world.

<margin>* Note.</margin>
46. And though it often happens that the children of God disagree with one another [or clash together] in their understanding, this proceeds only from the <term>Turba</term> <note>original: "Turba"; a term Boehme uses to describe the state of disturbance, confusion, or the "awakened wrath" in nature and reason that disrupts divine harmony.</note> of external reason, which is in all people. God permits this so that humanity might be tested and exercised; by praying and pressing toward God, one might more earnestly and fervently ignite his spirit. Then the Spirit of God arises in the mystery of *humanity* like a burning and shining fire, and all things must work for the best for the children of God.

47. But concerning certain people in your *neighborhood* whom you mentioned, who sell everything they have and run to a *supposed Zion* <note>Likely referring to radical religious groups or physical locations where believers fled, expecting to find a literal "Holy City" or escape impending judgment.</note>: I believe it would be better advice for them to stay at home, for *Zion* must be conceived and born within us. When they arrive at that place, they will find themselves just as they were before; they must still live under the yoke of Christ regardless of where they are.

<margin>☛</margin>
48. God is in heaven, and *heaven is in man*; and if a person desires to be in heaven, then heaven must be manifested and revealed in...

<summary>Boehme argues that true spiritual knowledge and peace come from surrendering the ego to the Spirit of Christ ("resignation"). He warns that "Zion" is an internal spiritual state rather than a physical location to be sought by traveling.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jakob Boehme’s discussion of true spiritual transformation. He contrasts genuine "resignation" (surrendering the self to God) with the superficial piety and intellectual pride he observes in some religious contemporaries. He emphasizes that true faith is an internal state rather than an outward display or a set of historical facts.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>

# The Second Epistle.

...in him; and this must be accomplished and brought to pass by *earnest, serious repentance, and hearty resignation*, or *unfeigned self-denial*. This they may do just as well at *home* in their own places; that which they think to run away from, they are likely to run into. It would be more acceptable to God to walk at home in a godly, divine way, so that others might follow their example.

49. There are among them arrogant, proud, scornful, and deriding people, who do nothing but condemn and despise others. In many of them, this is only a received form and custom—a spiritual pride, or selfish Pharisaic <note>original: "Pharisaicall Devotion"; referring to the biblical Pharisees, who were criticized for outward legalism without inner faith.</note> devotion, as I myself can speak from experience. For in a Christian, brotherly, and friendly manner, I besought and warned one of them because of a book which he published. In that book, I found some points of great importance against God and the foundation of truth. I hoped that he would gain insight, but he answered in a proud, contemptuous, and slanderous manner. He gave an answer in which no characters or marks of God's spirit were to be seen. Their *confession of faith* is an *opinion* rather than a true and sincere earnestness; for not all of them are what they boast and glory to be. There may be many honest hearts among them, but many are only historical <note>original: "Hiſtoricall"; Boehme used this to describe those who believe in the Bible as a record of past facts but lack a living, internal experience of Christ.</note> and *titular* <note>original: "Titular"; meaning Christians by title or name only, lacking the actual power of the Spirit.</note>. They desire only to show themselves off and to be applauded, as I myself experienced with one of the most prominent among them. They could learn at home to despise other men [without running to an outward, supposed Zion].

<margin>\* To revoke or recall themselves.</margin>
50. It is the way of children into God's kingdom, and moreover their way is \* *to recall themselves* <note>original: "Revoca"; Latin for "recall" or "withdraw." In this context, it suggests a turning inward or a retraction of outward errors.</note>. They themselves make a show of this, but privately they are as they were before. I wish to God they were as earnest as they pretend to be, and then I would commend them also. But to slander, condemn, and despise others is nothing but *Babel* <note>original: "Babel"; symbolizing the state of spiritual confusion and the prideful "city of man" contrasted with the city of God.</note>. The world is already full of such people; I do not follow after them.

<margin>\* Hans Weyrauck.</margin>
51. Concerning \* *Hans Weyrauck*, as far as I can see by these writings of his, he may be one who walks in the love of God—if his path is held in the real sincerity of the heart. However, the fact that he taunts and disparages others because of their knowledge of the <term>light of nature</term> <note>original: "light of nature"; Boehme's term for the innate understanding of the natural world and its divine signatures, which he believed was a gift from God.</note> shows that he has no knowledge of it himself, and his gifts do not reach that far. Because he lacks such a gift, we must overlook it; and yet, despite this, we should esteem him as a true and honest brother. For God produces his gifts not only in simplicity, but in many people in a high style [or in a deep-grounded understanding or <term>magical meaning</term> <note>original: "magicall meaning"; for Boehme, "magic" referred to the spiritual power and the hidden wisdom of God's creative word rather than sorcery.</note>]. For God alone is high, and he orders and directs all his works as he pleases.

52. In like manner, I answer the rest of the authors whom you mention, some of whom were endowed with high gifts, but they...

<meta>catchword: were</meta>

<summary>Boehme criticizes "titular" Christians who focus on outward forms and spiritual pride rather than inner transformation. He specifically addresses the writings of Hans Weyrauck, advocating for brotherly love despite differences in spiritual gifts or understanding of the "light of nature."</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jakob Boehme's theological defense regarding the nature of Christ. He addresses the limitations of past authors and critiques contemporary views, specifically those of Caspar Schwenckfeld, regarding whether Christ's humanity should be considered a "creature" (a created thing) or purely divine.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>
<header>The Second Epistle.</header>

<margin>☞</margin>...were not sufficiently capable of understanding everything; yet for their time, they did enough. But because the present time needs a different *medicine*, there are now other skillful, understanding experts and observers <note>original: "shewers"</note> of the disease. This is all according to God's loving providence, for He does not want anyone to *perish*, but for everyone to be *helped and cured*.

53. If those same authors were alive today, they might have written more clearly on some points and in a different style. Although they did enough for their time, they should not be despised or rejected, even if some points could be corrected. But their doctrine concerning the *union of the Deity and humanity is very clear*, and we can see how God's Spirit was in them. However, human reason turns everything for the worst; through its false explanations and logical interpretations <note>original: "Logicall glosses"</note>, it twists them into a perverse meaning.

<margin>* Schwenckfeld <note>original: "Swenckfelt"; Caspar Schwenckfeld (1489–1561) was a German theologian who taught that Christ's humanity became increasingly divine, leading to debates over whether Christ was a "creature."</note></margin>
<margin>☞</margin>54. * Schwenckfeld stumbles on this point: he believes that Christ is not a creature <note>meaning a created being or having a created nature.</note>. He has not yet understood the underlying principles, so it is impossible for him to distinguish how and in what way Christ is not a creature. In terms of His *Deity* (His divine nature), He is not a creature. But in terms of the *Heavenly Essence* (*about which He said He had come from heaven and was also in heaven*), He is "creatural" <note>meaning having the specific form of a created being.</note> within His humanity, but "uncreatural" <note>meaning existing in a universal, unmanifested state.</note> outside of His humanity.

55. Just as we humans live in the four elements and are ourselves made of the properties of those elements—so that they are "creatural" within us but "uncreatural" outside of us—the unformed, uncreated elements outside us (in which we live) and the formed, created elements within us are essentially one thing. It is the same in the Person of Christ.

<margin>☞</margin>56. The whole angelic world (which is the *second principle* <note>In Boehme's system, the "Second Principle" is the divine world of light and love.</note>) is His bodily being or personal essence. In regard to the heavenly essence in the Person of His humanity, it is *creatural*, but outside that Person, it is "uncreatural." For He is the Father’s Heart and Word, and the Heart is everywhere in the *Father*. So, wherever His Heart is, heaven is also there, along with the divine essence surrounded by the complete fullness of Wisdom.

57. Regarding His soul, which He committed into His Father's hands and of which He spoke on the Mount of Olives, saying *that it was afflicted and heavy, even unto death*—it shares the same properties as our own souls. It was for the sake of the soul that God became man, so that He might bring the soul back into Himself and draw our will back to Him, out of its earthly state. This soul is a creature.

58. And the *third principle* <note>Boehme's "Third Principle" is the physical, material world.</note> (which is the external kingdom...

<summary>Boehme discusses the dual nature of Christ as both created and uncreated, critiquing Caspar Schwenckfeld’s theology while explaining how Christ's humanity connects the divine and earthly realms.</summary>
<keywords>Jakob Boehme, Caspar Schwenckfeld, Christology, Deity, Humanity, Heavenly Essence, Second Principle, Third Principle, creature, soul, divine providence</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues Boehme's discussion on the "creatural" nature of Christ's humanity, specifically addressing the views of Valentin Weigel and the importance of internal spiritual transformation over outward religious knowledge.</meta>

<page-num>31</page-num>
<header>The Second Epistle.</header>

...of this world (which God through His wisdom has brought forth out of eternity) is also "creatural" <note>original: "creaturall"; meaning having the nature of a created being</note> in Him. For the whole Deity has manifested itself in the man Christ. **Namely**, just as God is everything in the spirit, He is likewise everything in this man. We humans are exactly the same if we are born again of God. This point—which troubles and preoccupies almost everyone else—could be easily resolved and corrected. If it were well considered, there would not be so much condemnation and arguing. The Spirit of God does not care for controversy; He judges all things within Himself.

<margin>☞</margin>
<margin>* *Weigel* <note>original: "Weigelius"; Valentin Weigel (1533–1588) was a German mystic and theologian whose writings influenced Boehme.</note>.</margin>
59. Also, * *Weigel* writes that Mary is not the daughter of *Joachim* and *Anna*, and that Christ took nothing from our human nature, but rather that she is an Eternal Virgin. This is indeed true regarding the "Mark" or "Sign of the Covenant," according to the Virgin of Divine Wisdom. But what would this benefit me? <margin>☞</margin> What would become of my soul and my heavenly essence <note>original: "essentiality"</note>—which disappeared in Adam and is the Image of Paradise—if Christ had not taken upon Himself the essence of our soul and brought that lost Image back to life? This is explained at length in my book, *The Threefold Life*.

60. Apart from this point, *Weigel* also writes very well about the new birth and the union of humanity in Christ, in agreement with us. I will omit speaking further of that here because I have written about it clearly and precisely. I neither look down on nor despise his writings, nor those who read them.

<margin>{</margin>
61. Does not a bee gather honey from many different flowers? Even though one flower might be better than another, she does not stop at that; she takes what serves her purpose. If the sap and power <note>original: "vertue"</note> of a flower does not please her, should she therefore jab her sting into it, as the scorner and mocker tend to do? <margin>☞</margin> People argue and fight much over the "shell"—the outward appearance of knowledge and religion—but they ignore the precious "sap" of love and faith, which is what actually brings life.

62. What good does knowledge do me if I † do not live within it and according to it? The knowing, the will, and the actual performance of it must all be within me. The mantle of Christ’s suffering and satisfaction <note>This refers to the doctrine of "imputed righteousness," where believers are "covered" by Christ's merit.</note>, which people now commonly wrap themselves in, shall become a snare and a hellish fire to many. This is because they only flatter themselves with Christ's merits while continuing in their clever hypocrisy and wickedness.

63. It is said, *“You must be born again, or else you shall not see the Kingdom of God.” “You must become like children if you wish to see the Kingdom of God.”* It is not enough to argue and dispute about knowledge and opinions; you must become a new person—a new...

<meta>This page continues Boehme's explanation of the spiritual rebirth and begins a detailed bibliography of his own works, which he offers to lend to the recipient.</meta>

<page-num>32</page-num>
<header>*The Second Epistle.*</header>

...creature] which lives in God in righteousness and holiness. The wicked one must be cast out, and Christ must be put on; then we are buried in His death, in and with Him, and we arise again with Him and live eternally in Him. Why then do I need to argue and wrangle about what I myself am? (Which I have essentially within me, and of which no man can deprive me.)

64. I am at odds with no one, except only against the wicked, and the spirit rebukes him to his face. I want to let you know this, as my intent toward you is sincere and upright.

65. As for my books, you may easily get them (I suppose) if you have a mind for them. For Christianus Bernard, a customs officer <note>original: "Customer"; a collector of customs duties</note> at Żagań <note>original: "Zagan"; a town in western Poland</note>, informs me that he has lent two of them to your butler’s brother. These are the book of *The Threefold Life* (which is the most important for teaching) and *The Forty Questions Concerning the Soul*. If you mention this to him, he will not deny you; but if not, then I will help you get them in another way. You may also get them from Mr. Christianus Bernard if you request them from him and cannot find them closer at hand. I will write to him so that he shall lend them to you, for I seldom have my own copies at home. However, if you do not get them, I will lend them to you one after another as soon as I can get them back.

66. The various books and their titles are these: the first book, called *Aurora*, climbs up out of infancy and shows you the creation of all beings. However, it is very mysterious and not sufficiently explained; it is of much deep magical, [cabalistic <note>original: "cabaliſticall"; referring to esoteric or mystical interpretations of scripture</note>], or parabolic <note>original: "pa-rabolicall"; using allegories or parables</note> meaning, for there are many mysteries within it that are yet to come to pass.

67. The second is a large book of a hundred sheets. It treats the *Three Principles of the Divine Essence* and the Being of all beings. This is a key and an alphabet for all those who desire to understand my writings. It discusses the creation, as well as the eternal birth or generation of the Deity, repentance, the justification of man, and his paradisiacal life. It also covers the fall, and then the new birth, the Testaments of Christ, and the total salvation of man. It is very profitable to read, for it is an "eye" to know the wonders in the mystery of God.

<margin>* Constellation, inclination, disposition, complexion <note>original: "comple-xion"; here meaning one's temperament or physical/mental constitution</note>, profession, and condition.</margin>

68. Thirdly, a book of *The Threefold Life*; this is sixty sheets long. It is a key to all mysteries, both above and below—to whatever the mind is able to think upon, or wherever the heart is able to turn and move itself. It shows the whole foundation of the Three Principles and serves everyone according to their own nature <note>original: "pro-perty"</note>. In it, one may find the depth and the resolution of all questions that reason is able to devise and propose. It is the most necessary book to serve your needs; you would soon be

<page-num>33</page-num>
<header>The Second Epistle.</header>

...weary of all contentious books, if you entertain and take that into your mind.

69. Fourthly, *The Forty Questions about the Soul*; it consists of twenty-eight sheets <note>original: "sheets"; referring to the physical size of the manuscript or printed sections</note> and treats all things that are necessary for a man to know.

70. The fifth book has three parts. The first part concerns the Incarnation of Christ. The second part is very deep and profound, treating Christ's passion, suffering, and death; it explains how we must enter into Christ's death and both die and arise again in and with Him, and why Christ had to die. This is fully presented, expanded, and confirmed from the Center, through the Three Principles <note>Böhme’s central concept of the three realms: the dark fire-world, the light love-world, and the external world of nature</note>, and is very deep. The third part is *The Tree of Christian Faith*, also demonstrated through the Three Principles and very profitable to read.

71. The sixth book, or part of these writings, consists of the *Six Points*, treating the greatest depths and secrets. Namely: how the Three Principles mutually beget, produce, and bear each other, so that in eternity there is no strife (or hostile enmity between them). Yet each Principle is within itself according to its own property, as if it were only one and alone. These points show where strife and disunity arise, and the origin of good and evil—all derived from the ground (that is, from the "Nothing" into the "Something") and all within the ground and center of Nature. This sixth book is such a mystery—even though it is presented with plainness and simplicity—that no human reason (no matter how sharp or academically learned an "astral head-piece" <note>Böhme uses "astral" to refer to the natural, worldly intellect influenced by the stars</note> one may be) can sound, fathom, or understand it without the light of God. It is the key to all the rest.

72. Seventhly, a small book for the melancholy <note>modern title: *A Consolatory Treatise of the Four Complexions*</note>, written for those tempted and afflicted in spirit. It shows where sadness and dejection of the soul come from and how they may be resisted and remedied.

73. Eighthly, a very deep book, *On the Signature of All Things* <note>original: "De signatura rerum"</note>, concerning the meaning of the various forms and shapes in the creation. It shows the beginning, ruin, and cure <note>original: "care"; likely used in the sense of the Latin *cura*, meaning healing or remedy</note> of everything. This book enters fully into eternal nature and then into temporal, beginning <note>original: "inchoative"; meaning something in its early or initial stages</note>, and external nature and its form.

74. These are my books, besides some small treatises I have given out here and there and kept no copy of. I have no need of them for myself, for I have enough in my "Three Leaves" <note>likely a metaphor for the three principles of his philosophy or the revelation of God in nature, heart, and scripture</note>.

75. If my schedule permits (for I must often take journeys because of my business affairs), I will visit you myself as soon as I travel that way. It was my full intention to have seen you at—

weeks after Easter, but God disposed it otherwise; by his providence I encountered <note>original: "light upon"</note> another man, who led me out of that intended path to one who had need of me; so that afterward I understood that my way was from the Lord.

76. Mr. *Balthasar Walter* <note>A traveling scholar and physician who was one of Böhme’s most important supporters and helped provide him with philosophical terminology</note> stayed this past winter and spring with Prince *Augustus* of *Anhalt* <note>original: "Tanbalt"; Prince Augustus of Anhalt-Plötzkau</note> at *Plötzkau* <note>original: "Peltska"</note>; and has written to me from there. <margin>* Ezekiel Meth</margin>
77. * *Ezekiel Meth* <note>A follower of the mystic Valentin Weigel</note> is also at the same court, yet they are not both of the same mind, as the letter from *Balthasar* shows, which I received three weeks ago. If you have a desire to write and no messenger comes this way, please send your letter to *Christianus Bernard*, the Receiver <note>an official in charge of collecting revenues or taxes</note> at *Sagan* <note>original: "Zagan"</note>; I have the opportunity to send to him weekly, and he is a pious Christian companion.

78. If you find anything in my writings that is too difficult or obscure to be understood, I pray you write it down and let me know. I will make it plainer for you so that you may understand it. For I have written nothing for the "wise" and "fully taught" who are prideful and advance themselves in their own knowledge—those who think they can walk alone and are already "rich" in wisdom. Rather, I have written only for the "babes and sucklings" <note>A biblical reference to the humble and simple-hearted who are open to spiritual truth</note> who nurse at their mother’s breasts and are eager to learn.

79. Let him who can understand it, understand it; but let him who cannot, not criticize or nitpick <note>original: "cavill"</note> at it. I have written nothing for such critics and mockers; I have written for myself.

<margin>80.</margin>
80. But if a brother thirsts and asks water of me, I will give him a drink; he shall find and feel through experience what I have given him, if the Lord grants him the drinking. I commend myself to your favor, and all of us into the pleasant and gracious love of *Jesus Christ*;

<page-num>35</page-num>
<header>The Third Epistle.</header>

# A Letter to
# ABRAHAM OF SOMMERFELD,
## Concerning the Book
# AURORA:

---

## ALSO,
### A Description of his process, and the excellency and surpassing Virtue of
### *Sophia's Pearl.* <note>The "Pearl of Wisdom" (Sophia) is a central concept in Boehme's mysticism, representing the divine light and heavenly wisdom that a believer must seek within themselves.</note>

---

> ***Light, salvation, and eternal power flowing from the wellspring of life, Jesus Christ, be our refreshment and comfort.***

<margin>1.</margin> NOBLE LORD, (first wishing you the grace of God, and all health and happiness) having been informed that you take great delight, love, and affection in my writings, which until now were unknown to you; I must answer you that this is likewise a much greater delight and surpassing joy in my spirit. This is because I understand that God drives and carries on His work in such great and high-ranking persons, which is not something commonly found in the world; for the worldly <note>original: "temporal"</note> honor and pleasure of this life is an hindrance to it.

<margin>2.</margin> But I can very well perceive how God’s Spirit has touched and awakened your noble heart. As proof of this <note>original: "in token whereof"</note>, you have devoted so much effort and expense to this work, which was written by a very simple and plain hand, without any art or great intellectual training <note>original: "understanding [or large capacity in literal endowments]"</note>, but only in the knowledge and manifestation of the gifts of God; and more—

<sig>F 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: over</meta>

<summary>Jacob Boehme writes to the nobleman Abraham of Sommerfeld, expressing joy that a man of high social standing has embraced his mystical writings. Boehme argues that while worldly status often hinders spiritual growth, Sommerfeld's interest in the book "Aurora" is evidence of a genuine divine awakening.</summary>
<keywords>Abraham of Sommerfeld, Aurora, Sophia's Pearl, Jacob Boehme, spiritual awakening, nobility and faith, divine illumination, mysticism</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues Boehme’s explanation of the origins of his first book, *Aurora*. He describes the work not as a planned intellectual project, but as a spontaneous spiritual eruption intended initially for his own private devotion.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>
<header>The Third Epistle.</header>

Moreover, it was not the intent of the author that it should come into the hands of such high-ranking persons; because he wrote it only as a memorial to himself, to stir and rouse himself up from the dark and drowsy sleep in flesh and blood, and not with the intention of making such a work.

<margin>☞</margin>
3. Indeed, there was a fiery prompting <note>original: "instigation"</note>, but without prior knowledge of this work; it lay hidden in him as a *mystery*, which the Spirit of **God** stirred up and awakened. At this, there arose a great longing and desire to write; and yet, in terms of the outward man <note>The "outward man" refers to the physical, worldly self as opposed to the "inward" or spiritual self.</note>, there was no desire, capacity, fitness, or ability in the author for it. For he sought only after the heart of **God**, to hide himself there from the **storm** and raging **tempest** of the **Devil**.

4. And he considered evil nature and its active influences, and often the deceit of the **Devil** and the anger of **God**, as well as the love and mercy of **God**. In this, many a storm and strong conflict were held against reason, and also against flesh and blood and the **Devil**. All this occurred under a powerful driving and urging of the Spirit, until at last a most precious **garland** or **diadem** <note>This "garland" or "diadem" refers to a spiritual crown of enlightenment or a mystical vision of divine truth.</note> was set upon his head, which this hand cannot describe in writing. Rather, I wish that the reader of this *Epistle* might find it through experience, and then he would understand what the sweetness of **God** is, and not marvel so much why a layman <note>original: "Lay-man"; Boehme was a shoemaker by trade, not a university-trained theologian.</note> should undertake to meddle with such things [or write of such *mysteries*].

5. Therefore, I say now that when the precious grain of **mustard seed** <note>A biblical reference to the Kingdom of Heaven starting as a tiny seed and growing into a great tree (Matthew 13:31-32).</note> was sown, this work was brought forth to be written. It was then seen as a *mystery* hidden very deep, without a full understanding, yet with exceeding joy. This is shown in this work (being my first book), where the great *mysteries* are set down very simply, without sufficient explanation or expression, and with much brevity and many defects—like a sudden flower that passes by, touching upon whatever it happens to land on; even so was the Spirit of the **Wonders**.

<margin>☞</margin>
6. For the author was an unlearned <note>original: "illiterate"; in the 17th century, this often meant someone not formally educated in Latin, the language of the scholars, rather than someone who could not read at all.</note> man, of very small understanding and shallow capacity compared to the learned, skillful, and expert. Indeed, he was like a mere **child** in these *mysteries*, who did not even understand the path he should walk or what might happen to him—except for what the Spirit indicated and declared to him. He has also set down in writing the persecution, disgrace, and shame <note>original: "ignominy"</note> that would fall upon him before it actually happened or before **reason** felt it or knew it through experience. This was shown as clearly as if it were already present, as can be seen in the book *Aurora*, which is the first part of my writings, composed before my persecution; and now it is a comfort to me that

<meta>catchword: that</meta>

<summary>Jacob Boehme describes the writing of his first book, Aurora, as a spontaneous spiritual event driven by God's Spirit rather than his own intellectual ambition. He emphasizes his own lack of formal education, portraying himself as a "child" in divine mysteries who even predicted his own persecution through the Spirit.</summary>
